North America
North America shows that adaptation responses can
differ as much within countries as between them,
shaped by the hazards faced and the strength of
existing prevention systems (Table 4). California
presents the strongest response in terms of financial
scale and structural prevention. Following the Los
Angeles wildfires, the state combined updated hazard
maps, expanded prescribed burning and fuel reduction
with support for home hardening, backed by USD4bn

for wildfire and forest resilience. Canada offers the
most developed community-based model. Its FireSmart
framework links local planning, risk assessment and
public preparedness, with dedicated funding for
Indigenous communities and public warning systems.
Implementation nevertheless remains uneven across
high-risk areas. Texas represents a more reactive
stage of adaptation. The Hill Country floods exposed
gaps in forecasting, warning systems and emergency

communication, prompting new investment in sirens,
gauges, meteorological infrastructure and disaster
response. These measures address clear operational
weaknesses, but place less emphasis on land-use
planning and long-term reduction of flood exposure. The
contrast reflects the nature and history of the hazards:
recurrent wildfire losses have encouraged California and
Canada to build permanent prevention frameworks,
while the Texas response followed a disaster that

revealed neglected local preparedness.

Sub-Saharan Africa
Recent climate events are driving two different stages
of adaptation in Sub Saharan Africa: anticipatory
support for slow moving drought and stronger
emergency management for sudden floods (Table
5). In Ethiopia, Kenya and Somalia, the drought
response reaches beyond humanitarian relief through
preparedness plans, cash support, solar-powered
irrigation, water access and livestock assistance. Its

design is increasingly preventive, but implementation
remains too limited for the scale of regional exposure.
The separately identified funding for East Africa and
Somalia also remains fragmented across programs.
Southern Africa has developed stronger operational
capacity for floods. Expanded forecasting, hydrological
monitoring and preventive evacuations helped countries
act earlier, while search-and-rescue operations and
drone assessments improved the immediate of response.

Yet repeated damage across Madagascar, Malawi,
Mozambique, South Africa, Zambia and Zimbabwe
shows that better warning cannot compensate for
vulnerable housing, transport and water infrastructure.
The difference largely reflects the hazards themselves:
drought develops gradually and allows earlier livelihood
protection, whereas floods require rapid warning and
evacuation. Across both regions, progress is most visible
in forecasting and emergency action, while investment

in resilient infrastructure and long-term risk reduction
remains less developed. Adaptation is therefore
advancing, but financing and coverage still fall short of
the geographic scale and recurrence of the shocks.

Middle East and North Africa (MENA)
MENA faces a dual hydrological risk: chronic
drought and water scarcity coexist with episodic but
potentially severe flooding, while preparedness and
policy maturity vary sharply across countries (Table
6). Morocco represents the clearest evidence that
sustained investment in early warning, hazard mapping
and disaster-risk institutions can reduce human impacts,
with large-scale preventive evacuations helping to

limit casualties during recent flooding. Tunisia has also
strengthened flood-risk management, but its response
remains more focused on post-event repairs, drainage
clearance and the scaling-up of protective infrastructure.
Drought adaptation is more challenging in Syria and
Iraq, where high exposure is compounded by structural
water scarcity, weaker institutional capacity and
broader socioeconomic vulnerability. Beyond domestic
adaptation, transboundary water cooperation is also

becoming increasingly important, as illustrated by Iraq’s
expanding water-management partnership with Turkey.
Overall, stronger preventive investment and cooperation
on shared water resources will be key to improving
regional resilience

2 of 8 September 8, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
MACRO STRATEGY
Commodities: From Inflation Hedge to Structural Growth Asset
Anna Snider, Managing Director and Head of Investment Manager Selection
(Due Diligence)
A broader role in portfolios: For decades, commodities occupied a relatively narrow
role in portfolios: protection against inflation when rising prices eroded the value
of traditional assets. That function remains important, but the investment case is

broadening. Commodities now sit at the intersection of geopolitical fragmentation,
supply-chain security, reshoring, inventory rebuilding, and the buildout of AI and digital
infrastructure. Together, these forces are turning parts of the asset class from purely
cyclical exposures into potential long-term growth beneficiaries.
The diversification case is also becoming more relevant. In Q1, when inflation concerns
peaked, the Bloomberg Commodity Index returned close to 25%, outperforming both

Equities and Fixed Income. At the same time, the relationship among major asset
classes has shifted. Equity and Fixed Income correlations have tightened in recent years,
while commodities have moved in the opposite direction: over the trailing five years,
their correlation with both Equities and bonds has been negative. That combination of
inflation sensitivity and differentiated return behavior can make commodities useful
beyond a simple tactical hedge.

AI infrastructure is a physical buildout: AI is often viewed through semiconductors,
software platforms, and hyperscale data centers. Yet the infrastructure required to
deploy AI is extraordinarily commodity intensive. It requires continuous electricity,
generation capacity, transmission lines, substations, transformers, cooling systems,
networking hardware and structural materials. In that sense, AI is not only a computing
revolution. It is also an energy, infrastructure, and critical-materials investment cycle.

Power is the first constraint. Data centers require reliable, around-the-clock electricity.
Natural gas remains a practical source of incremental generation in the U.S., while
nuclear power is emerging as a strategic long-term option for hyperscalers seeking
stable baseload supply. Solar and wind can add capacity and support decarbonization
goals, but grid reliability and storage become more important as power demand rises.
That supports investment in batteries, backup power and grid-scale storage, with lithium

and graphite among the relevant inputs.
The buildout also increases demand for industrial metals. Copper is a clear beneficiary
because it is essential to transmission, transformers, cooling systems and data-center
wiring. Aluminum and iron ore are used in facilities, electrical equipment, transmission
networks and supporting infrastructure. Semiconductor fabrication adds another layer
of demand through industrial gases such as helium and neon, high-purity quartz for

wafers and optics, and gallium and germanium for advanced chips, photonics, and
telecommunications. AI-enabled automation and robotics also depend on permanent
magnets that use rare earth elements, including neodymium-praseodymium and
dysprosium-terbium.
Demand has moved faster than supply: This demand surge resembles earlier cycles
in semiconductors and memory, where expectations pulled spending forward. Metals
and mining companies are already seeing earnings growth and margin expansion,

yet the industry has struggled to keep pace with demand. The imbalance matters
because commodity supply is slow to respond. Production of many strategic materials
is geographically concentrated, permitting timelines are lengthy, recycling capacity is
limited, and substitution can remain years from commercial viability. New mines and
processing facilities can take a decade or more to reach production.
Investment Implications
Commodities may increasingly serve

three portfolio purposes: inflation
protection, diversification from
traditional assets, and participation in
long-term infrastructure and resource-
demand growth, supporting a diversified
allocation across direct commodities
and select producer Equities rather than
a single directional trade.

### Page 3

3 of 8 September 8, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
Governments are also taking a larger role through industrial policy, export controls,
stockpiling and efforts to secure critical supply chains. Restrictions involving gallium,
germanium, graphite and other strategic materials underscore how geopolitics can affect
availability and pricing. As a result, the current cycle may produce greater scarcity and
pricing power than demand growth alone would suggest.

So far, this dynamic has been most visible in industrial metals, but the same forces could
extend into agriculture. Reshoring changes where food and inputs are produced and
processed, while inventory rebuilding can lift demand for fertilizer, fuel, machinery and
storage. Agriculture has not yet experienced the same earnings and pricing impact as
metals, but supply-chain resilience could make it a broader beneficiary over time.
Cyclical forces still matter: The structural thesis does not eliminate near-term

volatility. Energy remains a key transmission mechanism for inflation because oil and
natural gas underpin transportation, manufacturing and industrial production. Geopolitical
disruptions can cascade through supply chains, raising costs and moving both commodity
futures and spot prices. Currency dynamics matter as well. A stronger U.S. dollar can
restrain commodity prices by increasing costs for non-U.S. buyers, while renewed dollar
weakness could support emerging-market demand and reprice dollar-denominated

assets.
A stabilization of geopolitical tensions could create a more constructive cyclical backdrop
through lower energy volatility, improved business confidence and stronger capital
investment. Conversely, renewed conflict can produce sharp and uneven moves across
the complex. These crosscurrents reinforce the value of treating commodities as a
diversified allocation rather than a single directional trade.
Gold, implementation and the portfolio case: Gold is fundamentally different from

most industrial commodities, but it deserves attention. Its traditional relationships with
interest rates and the dollar have been less reliable lately. Even so, sustained central bank
and exchange-traded fund buying points to durable long-term demand despite bouts of
volatility such as the selloff earlier this year. Gold also tended to move inversely with oil
during the height of the Iran conflict, illustrating how different commodities can respond

differently to the same geopolitical shock.
Investors can access the theme through commodity futures or producer Equities. Direct
commodity exposure is generally the clearer vehicle for inflation protection, scarcity, and
geopolitical supply shocks. Producer equities can offer greater upside when commodity
prices rise faster than operating costs because earnings and margins expand, but that
leverage is asymmetric. Company-specific execution, financing, cost and valuation risks

can still cause Equities to underperform the underlying commodity. A blended approach
may capture both physical scarcity and corporate operating leverage.
Ultimately, commodities remain sensitive to energy markets, currencies, growth, and
geopolitics, but their portfolio role is expanding. They can provide inflation protection and
diversification while offering exposure to AI infrastructure, electrification, energy security,
defense modernization, reshoring and, potentially, agricultural supply-chain investment.

The strongest beneficiaries of the AI trade may therefore extend well beyond technology
companies to the materials and power systems that make deployment possible.

### Page 4

4 of 8 September 8, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
MARKET VIEW
Rising Global Interest Rates and International Markets
Ehiwario Efeyini, Director and Senior Investment Strategist
Strong earnings growth has so far delivered a fourth consecutive year of double-digit
gains for global Equity indexes in 2026, propelling them to new all-time highs. But a
growing risk throughout the year has been the increase in government bond yields, which

have also broken out to multi-decade highs across a number of the major markets. The
U.S. Treasury will this week begin its expanded Treasury securities buyback program
announced in mid-August in response to rising rates. But 10-year yields across all
the major G10 countries have moved higher, in many cases by as much as or more
than in the U.S. market. Against a backdrop of above-trend growth in nominal gross
domestic product (GDP), high energy prices and elevated inflation, investors appear to

be reassessing monetary policy expectations, particularly in Europe where most of the
2026 increase in 10-year yields has been driven by inflation breakevens (Exhibit 1). Fixed
Income markets are consequently looking for policy rate hikes from the European Central
Bank later this week and potentially from the Bank of Japan later next month.
The rise in global interest rates has also come alongside an expansion in fiscal policy
across the major markets. In Europe, 2026 and 2027 are expected to be the peak

spending years for defense and infrastructure expenditure plans unveiled last year. The
so-called Readiness 2030 package allocates €800 billion to increased European Union
defense spending as it looks to move toward its 5% of GDP target by 2035. And at the
same time, the German government relaxation of its national debt brake has directed
€500 billion toward funding for investment in clean energy, transportation and digital
infrastructure. Similarly in Japan, the government supermajority is looking to enact a

new stimulus aimed at raising investment levels across 17 strategic sectors such as
semiconductors, defense and shipbuilding, in addition to a planned two-year cut to the
consumption tax on food from April 2027. And in the UK, the new central government
leadership is also likely to announce new spending measures when its first national
budget is released next month. To the extent that increased public investment spending
can lift productivity and support nominal GDP growth in Europe and Japan, the rise in

yields need not undermine economic growth or derail the equity market advance. But
higher rates, especially in response to supply-driven energy price inflation, nonetheless
remain a risk for developed market equity returns going into the end of the year.
Exhibit 1: Global Bond Yields in Major Markets have Risen by as Much or More
than U.S. Yields in 2026.

For many emerging markets the picture looks quite different. In contrast with the U.S.,
Japan and Europe, inflation rates are generally well below central bank targets outside
a limited number of resource exporters in Latin America and the booming AI-linked
economies of Korea and Taiwan (Exhibit 2). As a result, policy rate expectations over

the coming quarters are stable or lower for several emerging markets (including China,
Malaysia, Thailand, Poland, Brazil and Mexico) according to Bloomberg consensus forecasts.
Investment Implications
Ongoing strength in economic growth
and corporate earnings should support
global equity markets even in the face
of rising rates. For Emerging Markets
(EM) Equities in particular, contained
inflation, flat to lower local policy rate
expectations and stronger fundamentals

should limit the impact. And we
continue to favor EM Equities over
International Developed Markets, where
inflation levels are generally still above
target, central banks are expected
to raise rates and investors will be
watching for fiscal sustainability as
governments implement expansionary
budget plans.

than EMs.

Historically, rising U.S. interest rates have been a major headwind for emerging markets.
But looking ahead in the current cycle, we still expect the impact of potential Federal
Reserve (Fed) rate hikes to be lower than in the past. Over recent years, the association
between tighter Fed policy and weaker EM returns appears to have diminished. EM
Equities for example were resilient in the face of aggressive U.S. rate increases in 2022
and 2023, broadly keeping pace with the rest of the world. This is in sharp contrast to

prior cycles in which tighter Fed policy has resulted in capital flight, deleveraging and
falling asset values across the emerging world. Particularly during the crisis periods of
the 1980s and 1990s—but also more recently during the 2015 taper tantrum—U.S.
monetary tightening has tended to attract capital away from riskier emerging countries
(particularly those with large current account deficits), reducing support for local asset
prices and exchange rates.

Associated strength in the U.S. dollar has typically been an additional hurdle. For those
emerging economies with fixed exchange rates, a loss of export competitiveness has
further undermined current account positions, forcing central banks to run down their
foreign exchange reserves. And where exchange rates are floating, foreign currency
debts have risen in local currency terms, resulting in widespread defaults, debt
downgrades, recessions and additional exchange rate depreciation.

But starting in the years that followed the 1997-1998 Asian financial crisis and
continuing into the current cycle, the fundamentals across much of the EM universe have
improved. Fixed exchange rates have been abandoned. EM foreign currency reserves have
roughly doubled as a share of GDP . The share of EM debt denominated in U.S. dollars
has fallen significantly, led by the private sector across households and corporates. And
for most countries, current account deficits have narrowed or disappeared entirely. As a

result, emerging markets have become much more insulated from the direction of U.S.
interest rates on a structural basis, and this has only been reinforced by the AI-driven
revaluation of key Asian markets in 2026. Greater concentration in Korea and Taiwan has
increased the EM weighting in the surplus Asia-Pacific region to 83% as at the end of
August—up from 81% at the start of the year and 71% a decade ago.
Above-trend growth in nominal GDP and ongoing strength in corporate earnings should

support global equity markets even in the face of rising interest rates. But for emerging
Equities in particular, contained inflation relative to central bank targets, flat to lower
local policy rate expectations and stronger economic fundamentals should limit the
impact from higher global rates. We continue to favor EM Equities over International
Developed Markets, where inflation levels are generally still above target, central banks
are expected to raise interest rates and investors will be watching for fiscal sustainability

as governments implement expansionary budget plans.

### Page 6

6 of 8 September 8, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
THOUGHT OF THE WEEK
The One-Offs Inflating Q2 Earnings
Lauren Sanfilippo, Director and Senior Investment Strategist
Headline Q2 earnings numbers look almost too good to be true. And in a sense, they are.
With nearly all of S&P 500 companies’ results reported, EPS growth is tracking around
50% year-over-year (YoY)—not just an acceleration from Q1 but a second consecutive
quarter above 20%. Chiefly skewing the headline number are massive mark-to-market

gains on private investments for a handful of mega cap technology companies like
Alphabet and Amazon that booked outsized non-operating gains tied to their stakes in
Anthropic, while Microsoft benefited from its OpenAI investment. Strip those out, and
adjusted EPS growth notches down to 31%. The point being, after being sliced and
diced, 30%+ is well above any long-run historical earnings growth rate.
The one-off nature of earnings strength wasn’t limited to investment gains. A second,

less-discussed boost was tariff refunds. As Exhibit 3 illustrates, revenues from tariffs
steadily climbed from early 2025 through mid-2026 until the International Emergency
Economic Powers Act (IEEPA) tariff ruling which initiated the refund process. As
refunds were disbursed, customs receipts turned net negative in June and July, with
refund payments exceeding total tariff collections. Customs and Border Protection cash
withdrawals of more than $100 billion in refunds have now been paid—60% of the $165

billion in total eligible claims.
The recipients: the “importers of record,” or the dozens of S&P 500 companies that
have disclosed billions in refunds over the past two quarters; Apple alone received $2.19
billion, adding $0.11 to per-share earnings to their quarter. Other disclosed refunds
include Nike with $986 million, Amazon with $640 million, and General Motors refunded
$500 million (although accounting treatment and use of proceeds has varied).

The boost of investment gains and/or refunds may not be repeatable in future quarters.
Perhaps more telling is that the median company’s revenue grew roughly 15% YoY, the
strongest gain in years, corroborating that there is operating momentum rather than
just an accounting mirage for Q2 earnings. Margins, too, as a percent of GDP are at
their highest back to 1950, hovering north of 22%, see Exhibit 4. That this is occurring
amid rising rates and at a time of geopolitical uncertainty/disruption, rather than a post-

recessionary recovery, makes the results that much more impressive.
The Path Forward: Questions remain about the sustainability of these elevated growth
rates, although consensus expects EPS growth to stay above 20% in both Q3 and Q4.
If achieved, every quarter this year would see 20%-plus EPS growth. On the back of
brighter outlooks from Corporate America in recent weeks, full-year earnings growth
will decelerate into the mid-teens in 2027, but that moderation comes off a high base

of 2026. For now, the breadth of earnings growth is the encouraging story as 10 of
the 11 sectors reported growth (Energy and Communication Services leading the way,
Healthcare the only detractor). That’s a reflection of strong core businesses growing
revenues into 2027’s tougher YoY comparisons.

Investments have varying degrees of risk. Some of the risks involved with equity securities include the possibility that the value of the stocks may fluctuate in response to events specific to

the companies or markets, as well as economic, political or social events in the U.S. or abroad. Small cap and mid cap companies pose special risks, including possible illiquidity and greater price
volatility than funds consisting of larger, more established companies. Investing in fixed-income securities may involve certain risks, including the credit quality of individual issuers, possible
prepayments, market or economic developments and yields and share price fluctuations due to changes in interest rates. When interest rates go up, bond prices typically drop, and vice versa.

Investments in high-yield bonds (sometimes referred to as “junk bonds”) offer the potential for high current income and attractive total return, but involves certain risks. Changes in economic
conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect a junk bond issuer’s ability to make principal and interest payments. Income from investing in municipal bonds is generally exempt
from Federal and state taxes for residents of the issuing state. While the interest income is tax-exempt, any capital gains distributed are taxable to the investor. Income for some investors

may be subject to the Federal Alternative Minimum Tax (AMT). Treasury bills are less volatile than longer-term fixed income securities and are guaranteed as to timely payment of principal and
interest by the U.S. government. Bonds are subject to interest rate, inflation and credit risks. Investments in foreign securities (including ADRs) involve special risks, including foreign currency
risk and the possibility of substantial volatility due to adverse political, economic or other developments. These risks are magnified for investments made in emerging markets. Investments in

a certain industry or sector may pose additional risk due to lack of diversification and sector concentration. There are special risks associated with an investment in commodities, such as gold,
including market price fluctuations, regulatory changes, interest rate changes, credit risk, economic changes and the impact of adverse political or financial factors.
Alternative investments are speculative and involve a high degree of risk.

in higher return potential but also higher loss potential. Changes in economic conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect your investments. Before you invest in alternative
investments, you should consider your overall financial situation, how much money you have to invest, your need for liquidity, and your tolerance for risk.

Economy 
Consumer Checkpoint: Strength in numbers 
09 October 2026

Key takeaways 
• Consumer spending remained very solid in September. Bank of America aggregated credit and debit card spending per 
household rose 6.2% year-over-year (YoY), or 5.1% excluding gasoline, both the third strongest growth rates over the past four 
years. Month-over-month (MoM) spending was more muted, especially without gas. 
• Spending shows considerable breadth: the vast majority of discretionary retail and services categories are showing growth. 
Moreover, this strength extends across income cohorts, with the ongoing recovery of lower- and middle-income households' 
spending growth. 
• With the winter months fast approaching, holiday spending is already increasing at a solid rate, though it's still early. In Bank of 
America deposit data through September, buy now, pay later (BNPL) usage is rising but not at a rate that is overly concerning. 
However, nearly one in every five households that use BNPL has a repayment of over $500.

Consumer Checkpoint is a regular publication from Bank of America Institute. It aims to provide a holistic and real-time estimate of US 
consumers’ spending and their financial well-being, leveraging the depth and breadth of Bank of America proprietary data. Such data is not 
intended to be reflective or indicative of, and should not be relied upon as, the results of operations, financial conditions or performance of 
Bank of America. 
A solid September for spending   
Consumer spending continued to look solid in September. Bank of America total aggregated credit and debit card spending per 
household rose 6.2% year-over-year (YoY), while spending excluding gasoline increased 5.1% YoY. Notably, both are the third 
strongest growth rates in the past four years. Seasonally-adjusted total card spending rose 0.2% month-over-month (MoM), 
while spending excluding gasoline was little changed. Overall, spending growth remains well above the 2025 average rate 
(Exhibit 1). 
Exhibit 1: Total card spending per household rose 6.2% YoY and 5.1% YoY excluding gasoline in September 
Total card spending growth per household, based on Bank of America aggregated credit and debit card data (monthly, MoM%, seasonally adjusted (SA)) and 
(monthly, YoY%, non-SA, right-hand side (rhs))

Most spending categories are growing  
One striking thing about recent card spending, in our view, is that it is well balanced across categories. For example, both 
spending on retail (excluding gasoline) and services grew strongly YoY in September, at 3.5% and 5.4%, respectively. Meanwhile, 
retail spending is showing notably better momentum than in 2025 (Exhibit 2).  
Exhibit 2: Spending is rising across retail and services 
Retail (excluding gasoline) and services card spending per household (monthly, seasonally adjusted, index January 2025 = 100)

Source: Bank of America internal data 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
Well balanced across “nice-to-have” and “need-to-have” retail categories  
Is the momentum in retail spending reflective of “nice-to-have” discretionary or “need-to-have” necessity spending? In retail, 
this is a nuanced question, as many categories sit in a gray area between “necessity” and “discretionary”. General merchandise 
stores, for example, sell both essentials like groceries as well as nice-to-have electronics or even jewelry.  
In Exhibit 3, we account for this by grouping retail spending into “more discretionary” (furniture, electronics, building materials, 
online retailers, clothing retailers and miscellaneous stores), “partly discretionary” (auto, health and personal care retailers, 
general merchandise) and “mostly necessity” (gas and groceries). Even within these categories the edges will be blurred, but the 
overall impression is that retail spending growth is widely distributed, with spending rising across all fronts. One caveat: “mostly 
discretionary” spending has been making a smaller contribution since June. 
Exhibit 3: Retail spending is distributed across discretionary and necessity categories  
Percentage points contribution to YoY growth by type of retail spending (three-month moving average)

Source: Bank of America internal data. See methodology for category aggregation definitions.  
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
Looking at the share of “more discretionary” retail spending categories that are increasing YoY underscores that growth is well 
balanced and broad-based (Exhibit 4). Non-store and online retailers and miscellaneous stores saw some of the fastest growth, 
while furniture and building materials were among the laggards, though other durable categories showed some strength.

For services, growth is driven mostly by discretionary spending  
In services spending, on the other hand, the boundaries between discretionary and necessity spending are often clearer. Travel 
spending (e.g., airlines, lodging) can be thought of as, by and large, discretionary. At the same time, utility bills, rent and 
insurance payments are necessities.  
And in services, discretionary spending remains the clear driver, accounting for over three-quarters of YoY spending growth in 
September (Exhibit 6). Leisure and housing services are major components of growth, though travel and restaurants also made 
positive contributions. However, as we noted last month strength in airline spending largely reflects price rises rather than 
transactions growth (read more in Consumer Checkpoint: Still sizzling).  
Exhibit 6: Services spending growth has been mostly driven by discretionary spending over the past year

Lower-income households’ spending outpaced all but the top 5% 
The breadth in spending growth across categories is also evident across income cohorts. In September, lower-income 
households’ total card spending growth was 5.5% YoY, while the equivalent figures for middle- and higher-income households 
were 4.9% and 4.8% YoY, respectively. The top 5% of households by income continue to show the fastest spending growth, 
5.9% in September, though this has eased somewhat (Exhibit 7). 
Overall, the divergences across income cohorts are much smaller than they were in 2025 and the first three months of this year. 
Just focusing on spending excluding key necessities (gasoline, food and utilities) doesn’t meaningfully change the picture of 
convergence across income cohorts (Exhibit 8).  
Exhibit 7: The top 5% saw the strongest spending growth of any 
income cohort… 
Total card spending per household by income tercile and the top 5% 
(three-month moving average, YoY%, SA)

Source: Bank of America internal data 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE

Exhibit 8: …though discretionary spending for the top 5% has eased 
some since June 
Discretionary card spending per household by income tercile and the top 
5% (three-month moving average, YoY%, SA)

Source: Bank of America internal data 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE

The strength in lower- and middle-income households’ discretionary spending is occurring despite necessity spending shifting 
from a tailwind to a headwind over the past year amid rising gas prices (Exhibit 9). In our view, a key component of why these 
households have been able to combine rising necessity spending with strength in discretionary is the labor market and a 
recovery in their after-tax wage growth (Exhibit 10).  
In September, lower-income wage growth eased to 4.5% YoY from 4.7% YoY in August, while growth for middle- and higher-
income households rose to 3.8% and 3.7% YoY, respectively. These changes were modest, and lower-income households are 
continuing to see faster after-tax wage growth than other cohorts, a notable turnaround from the “K-shaped” pattern we 
observed over much of 2025 and 2026.  
Exhibit 9: Necessity spending has gone from a tailwind to a

Should we be concerned about the easing in household spending and after-tax wage growth for those with the top 5% of 
incomes? For now, probably not, as in our view this spending growth is likely being driven by wealth effects rather than wage 
gains. Absent a sharp correction in equity markets, there is little reason to expect them to retrench their spending. However, 
developments here are worth watching as this cohort comprises a significant proportion of overall consumer spending. They also 
tend to make outsized contributions to several discretionary spending categories (Exhibit 11).  
Exhibit 11: The top 5% of households by income make up a disproportionate amount of spending in highly discretionary categories

BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
With summer gone, thoughts turn to the holidays and BNPL… 
Now that summer is over, the next big gauge of consumer momentum is how people spend during the run-up to, and over, the 
holiday season. It is early days, but September spending on holiday items ran well ahead of 2025 (Exhibit 12). Given the backdrop 
of firmer after-tax wage growth and breadth across retail and services categories, this is perhaps not surprising. However, some 
of this spending strength could be due to rising prices as opposed to more purchases.

A concern often raised as we move into the holidays is whether consumers will lean on less traditional forms of credit such as 
buy now, pay later (BNPL) to finance their spending. Frequent BNPL users tend to have higher credit card utilization rates (read 
more in: Do consumers have wiggle room to absorb higher gas prices?).  
Looking at the share of households in Bank of America deposit data with a BNPL payment, we do tend to see a seasonal rise 
over the holidays (Exhibit 13), though in past years, these increases have quickly reversed. Further, while the underlying trend in 
BNPL usage is upwards, there are no signs of a sharp acceleration. We do, however, see some evidence that BNPL is being used 
to finance more expensive purchases, with the share of households making repayments above $500 rising, albeit relatively slowly 
(Exhibit 14).  
Exhibit 13: 8.3% of Bank of America households had a BNPL

(Exhibit 16). Overall, in our view, the increase in the share of BNPL users heading into the season does not appear too 
concerning, though the rate of usage for some BNPL consumers does bear watching.

Methodology  
Selected Bank of America transaction data is used to inform the macroeconomic views expressed in this report and should be considered in the 
context of other economic indicators and publicly available information. In certain instances, the data may provide directional and/or predictive 
value. The data used is not comprehensive; it is based on aggregated and anonymized selections of Bank of America data and may reflect a 
degree of selection bias and limitations on the data available. 
Any payments data represents aggregated spend from US Retail, Preferred, Small Business and Wealth Management clients with a deposit 
account or credit card. Aggregated spend include total credit card, debit card, ACH, wires, bill pay, business/peer-to-peer, cash, and checks.  
Any Small Business payments data represents aggregate spend from Small Business clients with a deposit account or a Small Business credit 
card. Payroll payments data include channels such as ACH (automated clearing house), bill pay, checks and wire. Bank of America per Small 
Business client data represents activity spending from active Small Business clients with a deposit account or a Small Business credit card and 
at least one transaction in each month. Small businesses in this report include business clients within Bank of America and generally defined as 
under $5mm in annual sales revenue.  
Unless otherwise stated, data is not adjusted for seasonality, processing days or portfolio changes, and may be subject to periodic revisions.  
The differences between the total and per household card spending growth rate (if discussed) can be explained by the following reasons:  
1. Overall total card spending growth is partially boosted by the growth in the number of active cardholders in our sample. This could be due to 
an increasing customer base or inactive customers using their cards more frequently. 
2. Per household card spending growth only looks at households that complete at least five transactions with Bank of America cards in the 
month. Per household spending growth isolates impacts from a changing sample size, which could be unrelated to underlying economic 
momentum, and potential spending volatility from less active users. 
3. Overall total card spending includes small business card spending while per household card spending does not. 
4. Differences due to using processing dates (total card spending) versus transaction date (per household card spending). 
5. Other differences including household formations due to young adults moving in and out of their parent’s houses during COVID. 
Any household consumer deposit data based on Bank of America internal data is derived by anonymizing and aggregating data from Bank of 
America consumer deposit accounts in the US and analyzing that data at a highly aggregated level. Whenever median household savings and 
checking balances are quoted, the data is based on a fixed cohort of households that had a consumer deposit account (checking and/or savings 
account) for all months from January 2019 through the most current month of data shown.  
Bank of America aggregated credit/debit card spending per household includes spending from active US households only. Only consumer card 
holders making a minimum of five transactions a month are included in the dataset. Spending from corporate cards are excluded. Data 
regarding merchants who receive payments are identified and classified by the Merchant Categorization Code (MCC) defined by financial 
services companies. The data are mapped using proprietary methods from the MCCs to the North American Industry Classification System 
(NAICS), which is also used by the Census Bureau, in order to classify spending data by subsector. Spending data may also be classified by other 
proprietary methods not using MCCs.  
Store category aggregation for “more discretionary”, “partly discretionary”, and “mostly necessity” is defined as follows: 
More discretionary: furniture, electronics, building materials, hobbies, miscellaneous stores, nonstore retail, clothing. 
Partly discretionary: auto, health and personal care, general merchandise. 
Mostly necessity: Gasoline, food and beverage.  
We consider a measure of services necessity spending that includes but is not limited to groceries, utilities, and gasoline. Discretionary services 
includes but is not limited to charitable donations, leisure travel, entertainment, and professional/consumer services. Discretionary retail 
includes but is not limited to general merchandise, miscellaneous, clothing, electronics and furniture. It excludes categories like groceries and 
gasoline. Holiday spending is defined as items in which spending in the November-December period is usually at least 20% of total annual 
spending on the category.  Value and premium grocers are determined judgmentally on a proprietary analysis of merchants by equity analysts. 
The “more discretionary” retail category includes furniture, electronics, building materials, online marketplaces, clothing retailers and 
miscellaneous stores; while “partly discretionary” retail contains auto parts and services, health and personal care retailers, general merchandise 
and “mostly necessity”  retail contains gas and groceries. 
Lower, middle and higher household income cuts in Bank of America credit and debit card spending per household, and consumer deposit 
account data are based on quantitative estimates of each households’ income. These quantitative estimates are bucketed according to terciles, 
with a third of households placed in each tercile periodically. The lowest tercile represents ‘lower income’, the middle tercile represents ‘middle 
income’ and the highest tercile ‘higher income’. The income thresholds between these terciles will move over time, reflecting any number of 
factors that impact income, including general wage inflation, changes in social security payments and individual households’ income. The 
income and tercile in which a household is categorised are periodically re-assessed.  
Generations, if discussed, are defined as follows: Gen Z, born after 1995; Younger Millennials: born between 1989-1995; Older Millennials: born 
between 1978-1988; Gen Xers: born between 1965-1977; Baby Boomer: 1946-1964; Traditionalists: pre-1946. 
Any reference to card spending per household on gasoline includes all purchases at gasoline stations and might include purchases of non-gas 
items. 
Additional information about the methodology used to aggregate the data is available upon request.

COUNTRY RISK PROFILE
: STRONG ECONOMIC GROWTH AND LOW PUBLIC 
AND 
FOREIGN DEBT VS. LOW EXTERNAL 
LIQUIDITY AND 
A 
WEAK BANKING SECTOR

◼ 
 Strong factors support 
 Vietnam’s 
 overall country risk  
and economic prospects
 :  
i) 
a 
strong manufacturing export base that is moving up the value chain
 ; 
ii) 
s
olid comparative advantages that continue to attract large FDI inflows
 ; 
iii) 
c
urrent account surpluses and moderate foreign debt
 ; 
iv) 
a 
moderate government debt burden
 ; 
v) 
g
radually improving policy making
 ; 
vi) 
 s
trong medium
 -
 term economic growth prospects, assuming  
 that 
 the authorities will continue to implement structural reforms 
and strengthen productivity in the entire economy.

◼ 
 On the negative side,  
major negative factors continue to fuel some macro
 -
 financial risks:  
i) t
 he excessive domestic debt level of the economy (excluding the government) and fragilities in the banking sector 
 – 
this is a bad 
combination that fuels high credit risks and financial instability risks
 ; 
ii) 
 low forex reserves
 , which 
 have not much strengthened in recent years
 , 
 despite current account surpluses and notably due to 
resident capital flight. Forex reserves do not  
provide good  
protect
ion 
against shocks, while 
 Vietnam 
 is vulnerable to external shocks
 ;  
iii) 
 v
ery high exposure to physical climate risk.  
 C
oastal and river
 -
 delta regions, where population and economic activity are 
concentrated, 
 are 
 highly exposed to sea
 -
 level rise
 s 
and flooding
 .

◼ 
 Non transfer risk and currency risk
 : 
 V
ietnam  
 has enjoyed very 
 favourable  
 external account dynamics in recent years  
 until 2025
 , 
thanks to 
 large FDI inflows
 , 
recurrent 
 current
 -
 account surpluses  
and declining f
 oreign debt (
 which is moderate, at 
 less than  
30% of 
both GDP and exports). 
 This trend 
 has been  
interrupted 
 in 
 2026 
as 
current account surpluses 
 are 
 decreasing
 . Meanwhile
 , 
there is a 
persist
 ent 
 risk of 
 increasing 
 resident capital outflows 
 fuelling  
 some 
 VND 
 depreciation pressure  
 (
w
hereas  
 the potential for foreign 
capital outflows is limited
 )
. 
As 
the forex  
reserve cushion is weak
 , it would 
 not enable the

 to 
defend the dong for a long 
time 
i
f there is

 . 
However, so far this year, 
 the 
VND/USD spot rate and the reference 
rate 
 (Vietnam has a crawling band FX regime) 
 have depreciated only slightly
 , supported by strong exports and FDI inflows
 .

◼ 
 Sovereign risk
 : 
the government debt burden 
 is moderate and 
 will remain 
 so in the short term, even if the fiscal deficit is projected to 
deteriorate
 . The fiscal deficit could exceed 3% of GDP in 2026 due to support measures in response to the energy shock (estimated at 
less than 1% of GDP in H1 2026), but this will not endanger public
 -
 debt dynamics. Government debt should fall again in 2027 thanks 
to lower deficits and fast GDP growth. Debt was only 30% of GDP in 2025 (vs. 41% in 2019) and interest payments consumed less  
than 5% of fiscal revenue (vs. 7.2% in 2019). The government cover
 s its funding needs mostly in the local bond market, which is 
deepening gradually and not 
 highly 
 exposed to foreign investors. Government debt in foreign currency declined to about 25% of total 
debt in 2025 (vs. 38% in 2019); this is still substantial, but almost all this FC debt 
 is made up  
 of concessional loans from official 
creditors. On the negative front, high credit risks in the state corporate and bank sectors 
 are 
 very large contingent risks for the 
sovereign. Moreover, public investment needs related to climate change and infrastructure development will increase in the 
 medium 
term
.

◼ 
 Systemic credit risk
 : 
Domestic credit to the private sector is too large and still rising (141% of GDP in 2025 vs. 108% in 2019) while 
the banking sector remains poorly capitali
 s
ed. Credit risks and resource misallocation have long been exacerbated by opacity and 
supervision flaws, as well as the use of high credit growth targets to support economic growth (+15% in 2026). In 2022
 –
2023, banks 
faced severe funding stress and asset
 -
 quality deterioration resulting from the property and bond market crisis. The situation has 
recovered gradually since then. In the 
 short term
 , credit risks may increase amid higher energy costs and if  
there is  
a correction in 
the tech cycle. Some banks, especially small private ones, also remain exposed to VND funding risk (whereas banks do not rely  
much 
on 
 foreign
-
 currency 
 financing). Positively, reforms, including stricter capital
 -
 adequacy requirements, continue to be introduced 
gradually 
 in order 
 to improve banks’ creditworthiness (
 the 
 SBV plans to test the removal of credit quotas for the soundest banks 
from 2026).

Document 226: Eurozone _ Growth on track despite the turbulence (2026-09-24)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Eurozone _ Growth on track despite the turbulence (2026-09-24).pdf
Report source: BNP Paribas
Date: 2026-09-24
Pages: 2
Status: ok

### Page 1

8
economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoPerspectives / 3rd Quarter 2026 / September 2026
The bank
for a changing
world
GROWTH ON TRACK DESPITE THE TURBULENCE
Growth was stronger than expected in Q2 and is set to strengthen further in the coming quarters. According to our fore-
casts, it will reach 1.5% in 2027 after 1.1% in 2026. This growth is expected to be driven by investment and its knock-on
effect on exports. On an annual average basis, inflation is expected to stand at 3% in 2026 and 2.8% in 2027, peaking at

the end of 2026. The ECB is expected to continue its monetary tightening. At this stage, a single further rate rise, aimed at
keeping inflation expectations anchored, is expected in December. The impact of rising interest rates (key policy rates and
market rates) on public finances and private sector borrowing conditions will be worth monitoring.
GROWTH: BEYOND THE SHOCKS, A RESILIENT MOMENTUM
Growth in the Eurozone remained robust in the first half of 2026, at

0.25% q/q in Q1 and 0.33% q/q in Q2, excluding Ireland (where economic
activity is volatile). It was driven by Germany, where signs of a recovery
are becoming clearer (see Germany). Our baseline scenario forecasts
growth of 1.1% in 2026, followed by 1.5% in 2027 on an annual average
basis. This growth is expected to be driven by the strengthening of
trends already underway in public and private investment (particularly
in defence and AI) and in exports (trade in services and intra-zone trade

in goods). Household consumption is likely to be resilient too. According
to our forecasts, job creation would continue to underpin household
incomes, thereby cushioning the impact of inflation.
A NEW LEASE OF LIFE FOR INDUSTRY
The Eurozone is undergoing one of the most profound industrial trans-
formations in its history. This is giving rise to divergences between key
sectors but, above all, to new drivers of growth whose effects are beco-
ming increasingly apparent (see our analysis). Indeed, the signs of an

economic recovery are growing stronger. Business sentiment indicators
show a marked improvement in the economic outlook across most sec-
tors (Chart 2), driven primarily by electronics (AI, digitalisation), the
automotive sector and aerospace. European industry is thus benefi -
ting from strong demand for capital goods; a structural trend that will
continue to be fuelled by ongoing investment cycles in AI, defence and
electrification. The introduction of minimum European content quotas

in the draft law on the industrial accelerator, together with the Com -
mission’s intention to tighten the criteria for access to European public
procurement markets, will provide additional support for European in-
dustry in the longer term.
FOREIGN TRADE: A REPOSITIONING THAT IS PAYING OFF
Following a decline in 2026, linked to rising energy import costs, the
trade surplus is expected to rise to around 3.0% of GDP in 2027 (of
which 2% of GDP for goods and 1% for services). Growth in European ex-

ports has slowed compared with its pre-Covid trend and is being ham-
pered by the decline in Chinese and US markets. However, Eurozone
exports in volume rose significantly in Q2 (+3.4% q/q).
More importantly, since Liberation Day (April 2025), Eurozone coun -
tries have found significant sources of growth amongst other European
partners, which has enabled them to cushion the decline in exports to
certain third markets. This is evident in exports to the rest of the EU,

EFTA countries (Switzerland in particular) and, to a lesser extent, the
United Kingdom, Ukraine and the Balkans (Chart 3). The momentum of
intra-European trade therefore remains very strong. It is expected to
strengthen further with the anticipated investments in defence, AI and
electrification.
FINANCING: FINANCIAL CONDITIONS ARE EXPECTED TO REMAIN TIGHT
Financial conditions in the Eurozone remained tight in Q2 2026, accor-
ding to indices calculated by the ECB (including the BIG [Broad Inter -

mediation Gauge]). They are expected to tighten more significantly in
the second half of the year: i/ the rise in long-term bond yields, which
has accelerated since the outbreak of the conflict in Iran, is expected to
continue; and ii/ the ECB will continue its monetary tightening (+25 bps

9
EcoPerspectives / 3rd Quarter 2026 / September 2026 economic-research.bnpparibas.com
The bank
for a changing
world
in Q3, then in Q4). For the time being, the debt burden is still weighing
only moderately on public finances due to the long average maturity of
countries’ debt (8.2 years on average in the Eurozone in July), but this
burden will increase by 2030.
On the corporate front, the rise in interest rates has remained contained
(+2 bps since January 2026, reaching 3.9% in July), which has contri -

buted to the strong growth in new investment loans (cumulated over
one year, +9.1% y/y in July compared with 15.3% y/y in September 2025).
By contrast, cumulative net debt securities issues over one year re -
mained stable in July, whilst cumulative net issues of listed shares over
one year halved month on month.
Rising interest rates will continue to hamper new variable-rate
home loans to households (around 65% of new lending) more signi -
ficantly than fixed-rate loans, for which rates stood in July at 3.69%

(+4 bps m/m) and 3.36% (+2 bps m/m) respectively. New home loans,
which are stable in 2026 on a cumulative over one year basis, are there-
fore unlikely to see any improvement. A fall in real-estate transactions
could weigh on new consumer credit through a reduction in expenditure
on home furnishings and renovations, whereas consumer credit has
been fairly buoyant so far (year-to-date, +5.2% y/y in July compared
with +1.4% y/y in January).
LABOUR MARKET: RESILIENCE YET TO BE CONFIRMED

Employment in the Eurozone rose again by an average of 0.1% q/q in
the first half of 2026, driven by Spain and Portugal. Given the growth
outlook in these two countries, this momentum is likely to continue,
whilst business sentiment surveys in Germany suggest that employ -
ment is beginning to stabilise. Nevertheless, employment levels are
plateauing (France, the Netherlands) or even falling in other countries
(Austria, Finland), which is likely to slow the decline in the Eurozone

unemployment rate, meaning it is expected to remain close to current
levels in 2027. Furthermore, second-round effects of the rise in inflation
on wages cannot be ruled out: the ECB’s wage tracker suggests wage
growth of close to 3% by summer 2027 (2.9% y/y in June 2027, compared
with 2.6% in September).
INFLATION: A PEAK EXPECTED IN LATE 2026
Changes in wage dynamics will be a key factor in the inflation out -
look for 2027, whilst energy price rises are picking up again, driven

by the conflict in the Middle East. This rise is even sharper than in
the spring for gas (linked to restocking ahead of winter) and is likely
to feed through to electricity prices in several countries. We therefore
anticipate inflation peaking at 3.7% y/y in Q4 2026, before easing as the
energy component falls. On an annual average, headline inflation is
expected to stand at 3% in 2026 and then at 2.8% in 2027.
At this stage, the pass-through of rising energy prices to other com -

ponents of the consumer price index, particularly services, remains li -
mited. The alternative core indicators monitored by the ECB, including
the PCCI (Persistent and Common Component of Inflation, 2.1% in July),
remain close to the 2% target. Core inflation, however, is expected to
peak two months later than headline inflation (3% in March 2027).
Inflationary risks are nevertheless tilted to the upside. These are lin -
ked to food prices, which are expected to be affected by the energy

shock and weather conditions (summer drought, El Niño); they could
also stem from a delayed impact of energy price rises on underlying
inflation. The rise in prices of energy-dependent inputs (notably che -
micals and plastics) and those linked to the AI sector is expected to be
sustained. Growth, which we expect to remain robust over the coming
quarters, will also fuel underlying inflationary pressures.
MONETARY POLICY: TIGHTENING WITHOUT SLOWING (GROWTH)

Following two 25-basis-point rises (in June and September), we ex -
pect a third in December, which would bring the deposit facility rate
to 2.75%. It would then be in slightly restrictive territory (we estimate
the upper bound of the neutral rate to be 2.5%). This rise would be
aimed primarily at anchoring inflation expectations and, consequently,
at curbing the pass-through effects of inflation. Monetary tightening
would therefore remain limited. Indeed, the overshoot of the inflation

target would be temporary, and the operational framework adopted by
the ECB in June 2025 allows it to tolerate this, provided it remains brief.
However, it cannot be ruled out that the ECB might take further action
if the decline in inflation proves slower than expected.
PUBLIC FINANCES ARE CONTINGENT ON RISING INTEREST RATES
The fiscal impulse in the Eurozone is expected to be clearly positive in
2026 due to the rise in defence and infrastructure spending in Germany.

This will contribute to a widening of the country’s primary deficit (from
+0.9 pp of GDP to 2.8%, according to our forecasts). The improvement in
public finances is expected to stall in both France and Italy, at least in
2026, due to the impact of inflation on the debt burden via index-linked
bonds. This trend is expected to reverse in 2027 with less expansionary
fiscal policies. Nevertheless, the increase in the debt service burden
would fuel a further rise in the public deficit, which would thus reach

around 3.3% and 3.5% of GDP in 2026 and 2027 respectively (compared
with 2.9% in 2025). Against this backdrop, the Eurozone’s public debt-
to-GDP ratio is projected to exceed 90% of GDP in 2026 and reach 93%
of GDP in 2027, according to our forecasts.
Article completed on 15 September 2026
Guillaume Derrien
guillaume.a.derrien@bnpparibas.com
Thomas Humblot
thomas.humblot@bnpparibas.com

Document 229: Fed builds credibility with hike; one more likely in Dec

Source PDF: all report pdf/Fed builds credibility with hike; one more likely in Dec.pdf
Report source: 渣打銀行 (Market Watch)
Date: 2026-09-17
Pages: 7
Status: ok

### Page 1

Fed builds credibility with 
hike; one more likely in Dec 
Summary 
Event: The US Federal Reserve hiked its key interest rate by 
25bps to 3.75-4.0%, in a unanimous decision. This marks the 
first hike since 2023, reversing the easing cycle that had 
taken rates down from 4.25% in September 2025 to a trough 
of 3.75% by December 2025. The revised projections 
indicate the Fed expects to raise rates by an additional 25bps 
this year, hold rates in 2027, and cut 25bps in both 2028 and 
2029. It upgraded the long-run rate slightly to 3.2%.  
Market reaction: The US yield curve mildly flattened , while 
the S&P500 index fell 0.5%. The policy rate sensitive US 2 -
year government bond yield jumped 7 bps to 4. 74% as 
markets factored in  three more 25bps hikes by June 2027 . 
The US 10-year yield rose 2bps to 5.02%, its highest since 
2007, while the 30-year yield was little changed. The USD 
index (DXY) rose 0.6%, breaking past its 50- and 100-DMAs. 
Investment strategy: Today’s move realises what we had 
been flagging in recent weeks i.e. the rising risk of a Fed rate 
hike following Fed Chair Warsh’s Jackson Hole speech and 
recent economic data prints, namely: a pick-up in job creation 
and inflation over the summer and a sharp rebound in oil 
prices due to the re-escalation of the Middle East conflict.  
This hike helps the Fed rebuild credibility as an inflation 
fighter. Given the unanimous decision to hike rates this week, 
we believe there will likely be one more hike before the end 
of this year, and one more in H1 2027 as we expect robust 
growth amid strong AI investments and a tight labour market, 
keeping inflation elevated.  That scenario would still be less 
than the three more rate hikes money markets are pricing 
after the latest Fed move. We believe policy rates can return 
to the 4.0 –4.25% range by end-2027, with the possibility of 
cuts in H2 2027  as the impact of tariffs and high oil prices 
start to fade towards the second half of 2027.   
Today’s refreshed Fed policy rate path is mildly bullish for the 
USD index; so we revise up our 3 -month target to 100. We 
also revise up our 3-month 10-year yield target to 5.0-5.25%.  
 The USD rose, while stocks fell after the Fed hiked rates 
and signalled one more rate hike this year; ultra-long 
bonds rose slightly as the Fed rebuild its inflation-
fighting credentials

2026-27 and inflation projection for 2026; however, it 
still sees inflation cooling over the next two years

Standard Chartered Bank

Backdrop to the rate hike 
In hiking rates, Fed Chair Warsh reaffirmed the inflation 
threat to the US economy. US headline inflation accelerated 
to 0.4% m/m in August and stayed elevated on an annualised 
basis at 3.4% y/y. Energy prices , driven by the Middle East 
conflict, were a primary contributor. Oil prices have extended 
gains since the August inflation report, surging past USD 
100/bbl. Meanwhile, core inflation rose 0.3% m/m in August, 
beating estimates for a 0.2% rise . Also, US producer prices 
jumped 0.4% m/m in August, the most since May, and 5.4%  
y/y, further reinforcing the inflationary backdrop. 
The inflation report follow ed a stronger -than-expected jobs 
report for August. US monthly job creation has surged to an 
average 80,000 this year, from 10,000, tightening a job 
market that already faces a supply crunch from the Trump 
administration’s clampdown against immigration.  The 
combination of an acceleration in job creation and inflation 
over the summer, combined with a rebound in oil prices, 
supported our expectation for a Fed rate hike this week.  
Removing “a dose of accommodation” 
Warsh framed the decision to hike as removing "a dose of 
accommodation", rather than the start of an aggressive rate 
hiking cycle, and we agree with that characterisation. The aim 
is to bring financial and credit conditions more in line with the 
Fed's price stability objectives. To quote Warsh: “Our 
predominant focus is on the price stability side of our 
mandate. The plain fact is that inflation is too high, and has 
been for too long….This  summer’s inflation readings do not 
tell me that underlying trends have meaningfully improved.” 
He added that many categories in the last consumer and 
producer inflation reports are still showing price increases 
above 3% on a six- and 12-month basis.  
The unanimous vote to hike rates, contrasts with a 9 -3 split 
vote in July to hold rates. When combined with the pencilling 
in of one more hike by December, this suggests a majority of 
Fed policymakers have turned more hawkish over the 
summer. In doing so, they have helped rebuild the Fed’s 
inflation-fighting credentials and its independence against 
President Trump’s pressures to cut rates. This explains why 
long-end bond yields were stable after this week’s hike. 
Nevertheless, Warsh provided little guidance on the future 
rate path, as he held what was reportedly the shortest post -
meeting press conference on record for a Fed chair . This is 
consistent with his stated preference for less central bank 
communication. We expect more clarity once the Fed task 
forces submit their reports by year end. 
— Jonathan Liang, CIO for Fixed Income and FX   
Rajat Bhattacharya, Senior Investment Strategist

potential crunch from an immigration clampdown

聯友金屬 7610 TT (綠能環保) 
供應跟不上需求擴大 
投資與評價 
1. 3Q26 受惠國際鎢供應持續吃緊、鎢價維持高檔，加上屏東二廠新增
30%產能陸續開出，自售比重持續提升，預估營收季增 47.5%，毛利率
66.5%，EPS 27.32 元創單季新高。 
2. 2027 年成長動能由鎢價轉向擴產與產品高值化，屏南三廠、屏科廠陸
續開出，產品由鎢酸鈉延伸至 APT、氧化鎢、鎢粉及碳化鎢，自售比重
有機會挑戰 60%，預估 2026/27 EPS 83.77/109.27 元。 
3. 目前股價評價位於近兩年本益比 10～24 倍區間中段，考量非中鎢供應
鏈稀缺性、自售比重提升及 2027 年新產能接棒，給予區間中間偏上的
22 倍 FY27E PER，給予強力買進評等，目標價 2,400 元(22x FY27E 
EPS)。

重點更新 
3Q26 鎢價與自售業務推升獲利續增 
預估 3Q26 營收 28.43 億元（+47.5% QoQ） ，主要受惠國際鎢價維持高檔、
鎢酸鈉自售業務成長，以及屏東二廠新增 30%產能陸續開出；毛利 18.90 億
元（+44.8% QoQ） ，毛利率66.5%（-1.3ppts QoQ） ，營收與毛利仍維持高
速成長 。 營業費用1.88 億元 （+55.6% QoQ） ， 稅後淨利13.27 億元 （+40.3% 
QoQ），EPS 27.32 元創單季新高。預估 4Q26 隨新產能進一步量產、自售比
重過半，營收29.59 億元 （+4.1% QoQ） ，惟費用投入增加，EPS 降至 26.17
元。 
2027 年產能與高值化產品接棒 
預估 2027 年營收 132.79 億元 （+52.1% YoY） ，成長動能由鎢價逐步轉向產
能擴張及產品高值化，屏南三廠與屏科廠新產能陸續開出，產品由鎢酸鈉延
伸至 APT、氧化鎢、鎢粉及碳化鎢，自售比重有機會挑戰 60%；毛利 87.22
億元（+48.9% YoY） ，毛利率65.7%（-1.4ppts YoY） 。惟新廠與新工序投入
增加 ， 營業費用20.62 億元 （+202.8% YoY） ， 使營業利益66.60 億元 （+28.7% 
YoY） 、 營益率50.2%（-9.1ppts YoY） ； 稅後淨利53.09 億元 （+30.4% YoY），
EPS 109.27 元再創年度新高。後續營運重點將轉向新產能利用率，以及高值
化產品的提升速率。

### Page 2

訪談內容 
3Q26 鎢價與自售業務推升獲利續增 
預估 3Q26 營收 28.43 億元（+47.5% QoQ） ，主要受惠國際鎢供應持續吃緊、鎢價維持高檔，
加上鎢酸鈉自售業務成長 ， 以及屏東二廠增加的30%新產能部分陸續開出 ； 國際市場中國持續限
制鎢相關產品出口，尤其對美國與日本出口限制趨嚴，使國際客戶加速建立非中供應鏈，聯友以
二次硬質合金回收生產鎢產品，在競爭者有限的情況下，可望維持穩定的需求與產品售價。預估
毛利 18.90 億元（+44.8% QoQ） ，毛利率66.5%（-1.3ppts QoQ） ，雖自售產品比例提高，但
毛利率較 2Q26 高基期略降 ； 營業費用1.88 億元 （+55.6% QoQ） ， 營業利益17.02 億元 （+43.7% 
QoQ） ，營益率59.9%（-1.6ppts QoQ） 。預估稅後淨利13.27 億元 （+40.3% QoQ），EPS 27.32
元創歷史新高。除鎢產品外，公司亦持續推進循環再製鈷粉客戶認證與出貨，作為後續產品高值
化方向。

圖 1：聯友金屬獲利預估

資料來源: 國泰證期研究部整理

4Q26 營收續創高峰 
預估 4Q26 營收 29.59 億元（+4.1% QoQ） ，從上季大幅成長後仍維持季增，主要受惠鎢產品自
售業務持續成長，以及預期國際鎢供需仍偏緊，且公司新產能進一步進入量產階段，使單季自售
比重過半 。 中國掌握全球主要原生鎢供應 ， 出口限制使海外供應鏈短期難以快速補足缺口 ， 軍工 、
半導體及 AI 相關精密加工需求則持續支撐國際鎢價。預估毛利 19.21 億元（+1.7% QoQ） ，毛
利率 64.9%（-1.5ppts QoQ） ；惟營業費用增至3.00 億元（+59.6% QoQ） ，在於新的工序流程
投資持續投入，使營業利益 16.22 億元（-4.7% QoQ） ，營益率54.8%（-5.1ppts QoQ） 。預估
稅後淨利 12.71 億元（-4.2% QoQ），EPS 26.17 元；整體而言 4Q26 營收與毛利仍向上，但費
用增加使獲利較 3Q26 高峰略為回落。

### Page 3

2026 年鎢價大漲與自售比例增加 
預估 2026 年營收 87.31 億元 （+521.2% YoY） ，受惠國際鎢價大幅上漲、鎢酸鈉自售業務放量，
以及非中鎢供應鏈需求快速提升。中國鎢出口限制使國際市場供給趨緊，聯友具備廢硬質合金回
收、濕法冶金及既有海外客戶基礎，使鎢價上漲透過自售產品，直接轉化為獲利成長。預估毛利
58.57 億元 （+1,254.0% YoY） ， 毛利率由2025 年的 30.8%大幅提升至 67.1%（+36.3ppts YoY），
主因仍在產品國際報價及自售業務成長明顯；營業費用 6.81 億元（+452.3% YoY） ，營業利益
51.76 億元（+1,573.7% YoY） ，營益率59.3%（+37.3ppts YoY） 。預估稅後淨利40.70 億元
（+1,587.1% YoY），EPS 83.77 元創歷史新高，2026 年為公司材料處理量由加工大幅轉向自售
產品後，獲利結構明顯轉變。

圖 2：鎢金屬價格走勢

\

資料來源：Fastmarkets, 國泰證期研究部整理

2027 年產能與高值化產品接棒 
預估 2027 年營收 132.79 億元（+52.1% YoY） ，在2026 年鎢價與自售業務快速成長後，2027
年成長動能逐步由價格轉向產能擴張及產品高值化。公司規劃由既有鎢酸鈉進一步向APT、氧化
鎢、鎢粉及碳化鎢延伸，屏南三廠與屏科廠相關新產能預計陸續開出，鎢相關加工產能有機會擴
增倍翻，若產品認證與量產順利，可望提高公司在鎢產業鏈的垂直整合程度。預估毛利87.22 億
元（+48.9% YoY） ，毛利率65.7%（-1.4ppts YoY） ，在營收快速擴張下仍維持六成以上高毛利
率；惟配合新廠、新產品及營運規模擴張，營業費用增至 20.62 億元（+202.8% YoY） ，使營業
利益 66.60 億元（+28.7% YoY） ，營益率降至50.2%（-9.1ppts YoY） 。預估稅後淨利53.09 億
元（+30.4% YoY），EPS 109.27 元再創年度新高；2027 年營收與毛利仍維持高速成長，但費用
投入增加使獲利增速低於營收，後續營運重點將轉向新產能利用率，及APT、氧化鎢、鎢粉、碳
化鎢等高值化產品的提升速率。

### Page 4

非中鎢供應鏈稀缺性提升 
中國鎢出口限制使國際供應持續偏緊，聯友藉二次硬質合金回收與濕法冶金技術，受惠國際客戶
加速建立非中供應鏈，2026 年鎢價上漲、自售比重提升及屏東二廠新增產能開出，帶動獲利明
顯成長；2027 年成長動能則由格進一步轉向產能擴張與產品高值化，隨屏南三廠及屏科廠新產
能陸續開出，產品由鎢酸鈉延伸至 APT、氧化鎢、鎢粉及碳化鎢，自售部分有機會在 2027 年挑
戰 60%。預估 2026/27 EPS 為 83.77/109.27 元。3. 目前股價評價位於近兩年本益比 10～24
倍區間中段，考量非中鎢供應鏈稀缺性、自售比重提升及 2027 年新產能接棒，給予區間中間偏
上的 22 倍 FY27E PER，給予強力買進評等，目標價 2,400 元(22x FY27E EPS)。

產業概況 
鎢金屬需求持續由工業與國防驅動 
2026 年中國鎢總供給約 8.51 萬噸 W、YoY +12.4%，其中原生礦約 4.36 萬噸、幾乎零成長，回
收鎢則由高價刺激增加約 50%至約 3.0 萬噸 ， 使回收料占供給由2025 年的 25–26%升至約 35%；
換言之 2026 幾乎所有可快速增加的供給彈性都來自回收處理，使得非新礦山供應的競爭也明顯
升高。顯示高鎢價已開始抑制價格敏感度較高的傳統用途，但切削工具、航太與國防等剛性需求
仍維持成長。全球鎢需求主要仍來自硬質合金，應用於切削工具、礦山鑽頭、汽車與精密加工；
其中礦業與營建約占 2025 年全球 WC 市場 38.5%。 
國防需求的重要性在 2026 年進一步提高，美國與以色列對伊朗軍事行動、俄烏戰爭持續消耗飛
彈與穿甲彈用鎢，這類用途與硬質合金不同，使用後無法回收，使其對原生鎢形成永久性消耗。
因全球每年鎢需求約 10～11 萬噸，其中原生礦約占 65%、回收料約 35%，現有需求須依賴二
次資源補足。短期新礦與冶煉產能建置時間長，因此即使高價刺激新增供給，供需緊張仍不容易
快速逆轉。 
各應用來看硬質合金(Cemented carbide)佔比最大，主要用於切削刀具、採礦鑽頭、耐磨零件與
國防彈藥等。加工材/材加工品（Mill products） ：如鎢絲、鎢棒、鎢板等。鋼鐵與合金（Steel & 
alloys） 用於高速鋼、耐熱合金鋼等。化學品 （Chemicals） ：催化劑、顏料及化學試劑。其中 PCB
鑽針需求在近年也快速增加，據估計因 AI server PCB 變得更厚、更多層、更難鑽，高速低損耗
材料使 WC 鑽針磨耗大幅提高。AI server PCB 已朝 28–46 層甚至更高層數發展，而一般鑽針
原本約可加 工 3,000 孔 ， 在高階材料下可能降到約600 孔 ， 在相同鑽孔量可能需要接近4–5 倍
鑽針消耗。半導體需求在 4Q26 仍然偏強，其中第一類是半導體製程本身使用的高純度鎢/WF₆
等材料，另一類是晶圓、封裝、設備與零組件加工所使用的 WC 超硬工具。整體而言隨著 AI 
GPU/ASIC、HBM 與先進封裝擴產，使高純度 W 與精密 WC 工具需求維持穩定成長。  
圖 3：鎢金屬主要應用

資料來源：S&P Global, 國泰證期研究部整理

### Page 5

中國七成產能限制鎢出口影響大 
全球鎢供應高度集中，在2025 年全球鎢精礦開採產量約 8 萬多噸，中國占約75%，且中國同時
掌握從礦山、APT、鎢粉到碳化鎢的完整加工鏈，鎢的供應風險除了礦源集中，更包含中游精煉
能力集中 。2H25 開始中國開始收緊鎢礦開採配額並實施出口管制後 ， 非中國市場供給明顯轉緊 ，
價格也顯著提升。 
全球鎢市場主要依賴中國供應，因此中國價格大致主導全球定價；但出口限制與供應鏈安全政策
出現後，國際買家開始建立中國以外的礦源、冶煉及回收體系，使得國際報價持續走高，鎢回收
目前約占全球供給三成，雖然產地來源風險較低，但是既有供應鏈的產能瓶頸，使得回收流程取
得鎢的價格持續走高。雖陸續有些新礦場宣告將投入開發，但仍需兩年以上時間準備，舊有礦場
的重啟也面臨到回收率與經濟性的挑戰，且對於環境衝擊高未必適合歐美國家，目前最為環保與
的作法仍是從回收鎢開始加工。透過廢鎢回收陸續轉化為鎢酸鈉、APT、氧化鎢、鎢粉、碳化鎢，
將有利於賣方市場的精鍊供應商，邁向更高值化的末端產品發展。

圖 4：鎢礦蘊藏分布

資料來源：USGS, 國泰證期研究部整理

美國積極多元化鎢礦來源 
3Q26 美國政府承諾向 Elmet Group 投入 US$450mn，並搭配 DLA 最高 US$2bn 鎢戰略庫存
採購，政策目標已從單純取得礦源轉向礦山+APT/精煉+零組件的垂直整合。US$450mn 投資將
用於擴張美國鎢製造能力 、 取得長期原料 、 投資美國及盟國礦山與建立新的refining/conversion
能力；其中初期資金約 US$200mn。另一方面 DLA 與 Elmet 簽訂最高 US$2bn IDIQ 合約，最
低保證採購 US$150mn，品項直接涵蓋鎢礦、鎢精礦及鎢酸鈉；Elmet 待新增礦山、offtake 與
processing capacity 形成後才向戰略庫存交貨，以避免政府囤貨擠壓既有民間供應。 EQ 
Resources 規劃向美國供應 4,000 噸鎢精礦；但 Nevada APT processing facility全面恢復營運
預計要到 2H28，因此 4Q26 政策首先增加的是戰略採購與長約需求，而非立即增加APT 現貨供
給。這代表短期甚至可能強化非中國的原料競爭，但預計要數年後後才有機會逐步改善美國中游
加工缺口。

### Page 6

鈷金屬供給仍有短缺風險 
2025 年全球鈷礦產量約 31 萬噸，其中剛果民主共和國約23 萬噸、占七成比重，全球約 50%儲
量亦集中於剛果 ， 而中國又掌握全球七成以上鈷精煉能力 ， 因此上游礦源與中游精煉均高度集中 。
需求方面 2025 年全球鈷消費動力電池占比超過 65%，EV 仍是最大需求來源；雖電池高鎳化、
低鈷化以及 LFP 滲透率提升，會限制單位電池鈷用量成長，但 IT 電池的使用仍以氧化鈷為主要
材料。而長期更值得注意的是航空發動機、燃氣渦輪、國防設備所使用的高溫合金，以及硬質合
金與高性能磁鐵需求。高溫合金目前約占鈷需求 15～20%，工業工具、硬質合金與催化劑約一
成，使鈷逐漸從單一新能源金屬轉變成兼具新能源與戰略材料屬性的關鍵金屬；在供應集中及可
追溯要求提高下，循環再生鈷、高純度鈷粉與非中國精煉能力的戰略價值亦將持續提高。

公司簡介 
聯友金屬不依賴原生鎢礦，而是以廢硬質合金、鎢鋼及研磨屑等二次料作為鎢來源，經濕式冶金
回收後製成鎢酸鈉（Sodium Tungstate） ，同時回收鈷，形成WC/鎢鋼廢料、鎢酸鈉、APT、氧
化鎢的城市礦山供應鏈。公司目前鎢酸鈉年產能約 6,000 噸，2025 年鎢酸鈉出貨約 4,728 噸、
YoY +8%，其中代工約占 52%、自售約 48%，相較 2024 年代工 42%、自售 58%，可看出自
有設備利用率已處相對高檔 ； 公司規劃透過新廠將鎢酸鈉產能進一步提高至約 12,000 噸/年、較
現有產能增加 100%， 若以鎢酸鈉Na₂WO₄·2H₂O 含鎢重量約 55.7%換算 ， 滿產相當於一年可處
理約 6,700 噸 W contained。公司並規劃進一步向下游 APT 與氧化鎢延伸，使產品由目前以鎢
酸鈉為主，逐步建立回收料、鎢酸鈉、APT、WO₃的垂直整合能力。

### Page 7

ESG 政策 
公司在社會面評分上屬中上，然而需在經濟與環境面與揭露面強化:  
經濟面: 股東權益方面，公司優點為董事長親自出席股東常會；股東會結束後公開揭露逐項議案
之詳細表決資訊。公司可加強處為當年度未召開 2 次以上法人說明會。 
法規遵循方面，公司於題項評估期間未有不合理或受主管機關處分之情事。獨立董事方面，公司
優點為至少 2 名獨立董事之連續任期不超過 9 年。公司可加強處為獨立董事席次未達董事席次
二分之一以上。董事會永續治理方面，公司優點為有設置永續發展相關委員會；有制訂環境、社
會及治理之中長期發展策略；當年度永續報告書經董事會通過。公司可加強處為負責永續發展之
最終負責人非董事長或總經理。董事會績效與薪酬方面，公司優點為將董事績效評估與薪資報酬
連結。公司可加強處為企業永續績效尚未與高階主管及董事薪酬進行連結。 
董事會多元性方面，公司優點為揭露董事會多元化政策並揭露落實情形；董事會成員具備不同專
業背景 。 公司可加強處為任一性別董事席次未達1/3。 永續創新方面 ， 公司優點為取得綠色認證 。
公司可加強處為未揭露綠色相關部門設置情形與職責；未揭露綠色/永續採購之政策或金額。 
此外，產業創新方面，公司優點為對其專利之智慧財產權進行管理與保護，並揭露量化績效。 
法規遵循方面，公司近 3 年並未受公平交易委員會懲罰。

環境面: 環境系統與治理方面，公司優點為積極將綠色概念融入營運過程，打造綠色價值鏈；榮
獲 ISO 14001 環境管理認證；將環境管理作為與聯合國永續發展目標(SDGs)連結。空氣管理方
面，公司優點為制定空氣管理政策、揭露空氣汙染排放數據；公司可加強處為未揭露量化空氣汙
染減量目標。法規遵循方面，評估期間未違反空氣汙染相關法規或已針對違反情形提出具體改善
措施。氣候變遷管理方面，公司可加強處為未說明董事會或管理階層對氣候變遷議題之監督頻率
或事項；未揭露使用具公信力的 1.5 °C 以下及 3 °C 以上溫度情境評估氣候風險；未揭露與供
應鏈或合作夥伴共同推動因應氣候變遷相關策略；未揭露氣候變遷對公司所產生的機會項目。 
溫室氣體管理方面，公司優點為揭露至少三年的溫室氣體範疇一與二排放量，並揭露涵蓋範圍或
佔營收比率。公司可加強處為未揭露溫室氣體管理專責單位及相關管理政策；未揭露範疇三排放
量；未揭露量化溫室氣體減量目標；未揭露是否通過碳盤查認證。節能管理方面，公司優點為揭
露用電或節能數據。公司可加強處為未揭露能源管理專責單位及相關管理政策；未揭露節能量化
目標；未揭露能源風險對營運的影響評估結果。再生能源管理方面，公司可加強處為未揭露再生
能源管理專責單位及相關推動政策；未揭露再生能源使用量或發電量；未揭露量化再生能源發展
目標。廢水管理方面，公司優點為制定廢水管理政策、揭露廢污水排放量；公司可加強處為未揭
露量化廢汙水減量目標。 
揭路面: ESG 揭露方面，公司優點為依據 GRI 準則編寫永續報告書；於永續報告書清楚說明揭露
範疇；建置英文網站，並包含財務、業務及公司治理相關資訊；於永續報告書揭露重大性分析細
節。公司可加強處為永續報告書未依據 SASB 進行對應揭露；未上傳英文版永續報告書於公開資
訊觀測站；永續報告書尚未經過第三方機構確信或保證；未於官網揭露利害關係人問卷，供利害
關係人回饋；未說明公司營運對應SDGs 目標之具體作為；未揭露使用雙重重大性分析重大性議
題。

市場投資展望
資產 4Q26看法 投資展望
金融 正向 AI驅動股債與併購復甦，資本市場將迎來週期中期健康成長，存貸款業務成長延續
1
《人工智慧(AI)基礎設施公司融資需求和市值的成
長在近期投資銀行收入成長中發揮了核心作用》
高盛預期投資銀行三大業務領域均會實現成長 ，並
處於週期中期。在諮詢業務方面，預期未來12個月
併購交易量將成長 19%；股權資本市場(ECM)方面，
近期成長主要得益於強勁的科技業 (尤其是 AI相關
產業)活動，其中科技類 ECM貢獻了年初至今成長
的約75%，高盛預期成長將持續 ，估計2026年美
國IPO籌資額將達到 2,250億美元的年度新高紀錄；
而債權資本市場 (DCM)的增長主要會由 2大因素推
動，(1)AI相關融資規模龐大 ，其中相當一部分預

計將轉化為 DCM的業務量 ，(2)基於對企業併購交
易量持正向看法 ，這亦將為 DCM提供強大支撐 。
因併購活動中有很大一部份會依賴債務融資。
高盛預估今年迄今為止，併購、股權資本市場和債
權資本市場交易量及費用的合計增長中，約有
分析師／陳嘉琳
研究範疇 金融／非核心消費
金融產業投資攻略
出 刊 日 : 2026/9/11
資料來源：GS，2026/9/8，中國信託商業銀行整理，2026/9/9
預計2026年美國IPO總募資額將創新高紀錄
美國IPO總募資額(單位：10億美元)

吸納量
重點摘要
2
50~55%是由AI相關交易驅動的 。眼下Claude開發
商Anthropic何時上市就成為市場焦點 ，目前市場
推估最快10月中展開首次公開發行(IPO)的推介活動，
並可能在11月美國期中選舉前數日掛牌上市 。此案
被 視 為 史 上 規 模 最 大 的 IPO 案之一 ， 緊 接 在
Anthropic之後還會有OpenAI。
《市場預期股票市場成長依然強勁 ，固定收益 、外
匯及大宗商品(FICC)市場成長放緩》
銀行交易收入能否延續今年上半年強勁表現 ，其持
續性取決於市場水平 、融資利差和波動性 。從結構
上來看，可交易流通股數的增長帶動了交易量 ，進
而推升了客戶資金融通該持股所需的槓桿額度 ，通

常透過大宗經紀業務進行。
正向的週期性效益包括：股票融資利差是 2020年第
三季的三倍 ，反映出股票槓桿需求已超越產業產能；
首度公開發行(IPO)的回溫活絡了次級市場交易；個
股波動率升幅高於大盤指數波動率 ，為客戶創造更
大的超額報酬(Alpha)機會並提升參與度。故高盛預
期2026~2028年美國大型銀行股票市場複合年增長
率將達到 5~7%，但同期間 FICC市場的複合年增長
約為2~4%，這是因為市場波動性較低 ，限制了中
介活動。
《規模效應 、資產負債表彈性驅動資本市場收入步
入健康增長》
自 2019 年 以 來 資 本 市 場 收 入 加 速 成 長， 2019 至
2026年產業費用池複合年增率 (CAGR)將達 11%，
高盛預測 2026至2028年將放緩至 4%，雖說放緩但
仍維持健康增長 。這項成長主要歸功於可交易資產
池的擴張 ，以及全球債務和股權市場規模的擴大 ，
而非利潤率的提高，大型銀行交易資產自 2019年以
來成長約 110%，同期利潤率基本持平 。此外，自

2025年11月補充槓桿率 (SLR)改革實施以來 ，銀行
受惠於資產負債表更大的彈性 ，槓桿率 (平均資產 /
權益)在2019年到2026年上半年明顯上升 。同時，
經營槓桿上升帶來規模經濟效益 ，使資本市場業務
的效率比率 (營運成本/收入)下降，這是因為更高的
固定成本轉化為規模經濟效益 。最終，使資本市場
業務的股東權益報酬率(ROE)上升。

Document 394: インド消費者物価（26年９月）～総合ＣＰＩは5.5％に上昇、食品高が一段と加速

Source PDF: all report pdf/インド消費者物価（26年９月）～総合ＣＰＩは5.5％に上昇、食品高が一段と加速.pdf
Report source: NLI
Date: 2026-10-13
Pages: 3
Status: ok

### Page 1

１．総合ＣＰＩは5.5％に上昇、農村部では6％台へ 
インド統計・計画実施省が10月12日に公表した2026年9月の消費者物価指数（以下、ＣＰＩ）に
よると、総合ＣＰＩ上昇率は前年同月比5.5％となり、前月の4.8％から上昇した（図表１）。総合
インフレ率はインド準備銀行（ＲＢＩ）の物価目標である4％を4カ月連続で上回り、許容レンジの
上限である6％に近づいた。 
地域別にみると、都市部では前年同月比4.9％（前月：4.3％）、農村部では同6.1％（同5.2％）
となり、 いずれも前月から上昇した。 とりわけ農村部ではインフレ率がＲＢＩの許容レンジの上限
である6％を上回り、都市部との差も前月の0.9％ポイントから1.2％ポイントに拡大した。 
９月のＣＰＩは、野菜・豆類を中心に食品価格の上昇が加速したほか、外食・宿泊サービスや交
通など幅広い分野で伸びが高まっており、 物価上昇圧力が一段と強まったことを示す内容となった。
一方、貴金属価格の影響が大きい身の回り品・その他の伸びは鈍化し、保健医療など一部のサービ
ス価格はなお低い伸びにとどまっている。 今後は、 食品や輸送コストの上昇が幅広い品目に波及し、
基調的なインフレ圧力をさらに強めるかが焦点となろう。

２．食品インフレは7％台後半に加速、野菜・豆類が急上昇 
食品・飲料の上昇率は前年同月比7.4％となり、前月の同5.7％から大きく加速した（図表１）。
このうち消費者食品価格指数（ＣＦＰＩ）は同7.7％となり、前月の同6.0％から上昇した。 
食品の主要項目別にみると、砂糖・菓子類が前年同月比13.8％、果物が同10.8％、調理食品が同
10.0％と二桁の高い伸びを示したほか、野菜・豆類が同9.2％、肉類が同8.7％、油脂が同8.3％、

インド消費者物価（26 年９月） 
～総合ＣＰＩは 5.5％に上昇、食品高が一段と加速

### Page 2

３．燃料・光熱費は伸び拡大、原油価格は再び上昇 
燃料・光熱費の上昇率は前年同月比 3.2％となり、
前月の同3.1％からやや上昇した（図表１）。エネル
ギー価格の上昇率はなお比較的低いものの、これ
まで総合ＣＰＩを抑制してきた効果は弱まりつつあ
る。 
インド原油バスケット価格は、２月末の中東情勢
の緊迫化を受けて３月に急騰した後、いったん低
下したものの、７月以降は米国とイランの対立再燃
などを背景に再び上昇し、直近では１バレル＝120
ドル台の高水準に達している（図表３）。為替相場も
26年央にかけてルピー安が進んだ後、足元では１ドル＝95ルピー前後の安値圏で推移しており、原油高
が続けば、輸入コストを通じて国内の燃料価格に上昇圧力が及ぶ可能性がある。 
一方、輸送関連では高めの伸びが続いている。交通全体の上昇率は前年同月比5.0％となり、前月の同
4.6％から上昇した。内訳をみると、自家用車などの維持・利用費は同7.5％、貨物輸送サービスは同
14.7％と高い伸びを示したほか、旅客輸送サービスも同3.8％と前月の同3.4％から上昇した。一方、輸送
機器の購入は同▲2.6％と下落が続いた。今後、原油高が長期化すれば、燃料価格だけでなく、物流費な
どを通じて食品やサービス価格にも上昇圧力が波及する可能性がある。

４．コアインフレは4.3％に上昇、物価上昇の裾野が広がる 
食品・飲料および燃料・光熱費を除くコアイ
ンフレは、前年同月比4.3％となり、前月の同
4.2％から小幅に上昇した（図表１）。 
分野別にみると、身の回り品・その他の財・
サービスや外食・宿泊サービスが引き続き高い
伸びを示した（図表４）。このほか、交通は前
年同月比5.0％（前月：4.6％）、衣料・履物は
同3.9％ （同3.6％） 、 教育サービスは同3.9％ （同
3.7％） 、 情報通信は同2.4％ （同2.0％） 、 娯楽・
スポーツ・文化は同2.2％（同1.7％）と、いずれも前月から伸びが高まった。一方、保健医療は同
1.4％と低い伸びにとどまった。

### Page 3

５．ＲＢＩは10月に利上げ、物価上振れリスクへの警戒を強める 
９月は総合ＣＰＩ、食品・飲料、コアＣＰＩ
の上昇率が揃って高まり、物価上昇圧力は前月
より一段と強まった。先行きは、降雨不足が食
品価格に及ぼす影響に加え、原油高によるコス
ト上昇が幅広い品目に波及するか が焦点となる。 
食品価格の先行きを左右する南西モンスーン
の降雨量は、６～９月の累計で平年の約87％に
とどまった。一方、カリフ作物の作付面積は10
月２日時点で約 1.12 億ヘクタールとなり、 平年
を約１％上回ったものの、前年同期を約１％下回った。作付けはおおむね平年水準を確保したが、
降雨不足や地域的なばらつきが収穫量に及ぼす影響に加え、 今後の冬作物の作付けへの影響にも注
意が必要である。 
こうしたなか、 ＲＢＩは10 月７日の金融政策決定会合で政策金利を 0.25％引き上げて 5.50％と
し、金融政策スタンスを「中立」から「調整された引き締め」に変更した。ＲＢＩは降雨不足や原
油高、 物価上昇圧力の広がりを警戒し、2026/27 年度のＣＰＩ上昇率予測を 5.0％から 5.2％に引き
上げた。 ９月のインフレ率上昇により、利上げ後の政策金利との差でみた実質金利はほぼゼロ％と
なった（図表５） 。 
もっとも、足元の物価上昇には食品や原油など供給面の要因が大きく、 基調的な需要インフレが
全面的に強まっているとは言い切れない。 ＲＢＩは10～12 月期のＣＰＩ上昇率を 6.0％と予測して
おり、当面は物価上昇圧力の広がりやコスト上昇の二次的な波及を慎重に見極めるとみられる。 食
品インフレの高止まりや原油高が続き、コアインフレへの波及が一段と明確になれば、 追加利上げ
観測が強まる可能性がある。

### Page 1

1． 結果の概要：総合指数、コア指数ともに前年比でわずかに上昇 
10月9日、ロシア連邦統計局は消費者物価指数を公表し、結果は以下の通りとなった。 
【総合指数（26年9月）】 
・前年同月比は6.35％、市場予想1（6.30％）から上振れ、前月（6.33％）からやや上昇（図表1） 
・前月比は0.35％、市場予想（0.30％）から上振れ、前月（▲0.08％）からプラスに転じる 
【コア指数2（26年 9月）】 
・前年同月比は5.47％、前月（5.37％）から上昇した（図表2） 
・前月比は0.49％、前月（0.29％）から上昇した 
（図表 1）

（図表 2）

2． 結果の詳細：直近は 10月 1日の公共料金引き上げが物価を大きく押し上げ 
9 月のロシアのインフレ率は 6.35％となり、8月（6.33％）からごくわずかに上昇、市場予想
（6.30％）から上振れた。ロシア中銀による季調済前月比の年率換算は6.5％となり、8月（7.5％）
から減速した。 
前年比インフレ率を大分類別に見ると、9月は食料品が 5.05％（前月：5.08％）、財（非食料品）
が 6.47％（前月：6.51％）、サービスが 7.97％（前月：7.85％）となり、食料品と財（非食料品）
でやや低下、サービスでやや上昇した。 
前年比寄与度では食料品が 2.0％ポイント程度、財（非食料品）が 2.1％ポイント程度、サービ

1 Bloomberg集計の中央値。以下の予想値も同様。 
2 生鮮食品など季節的要因による影響を受ける品目や管理品目を除いた指数。

### Page 2

（図表 4）

ロシア中央銀行が公表する家計のインフレ期待（1年先中央値、実際のインフレ率よりも高めに
なる傾向がある）は、9月は14.2％で 8月（13.7％）から上昇した。期待インフレ率と実際のイン
フレ率の乖離は過去の傾向（期待インフレ率≒前年比インフレ率＋6％、図表5）と比較してやや開
いており、9月は前月からさらに拡大した。 
品目別の上昇率を見ると3（図表 6）、9月は前
年比でガソリン（ 22.71％）、保険サービス
（18.73％）、グラニュー糖（ 17.38％）、卵
（17.20％）の伸び率が高い一方、バター（▲
7.96％）は相対的にマイナス幅が大きかった。
また前月比では、教育サービス（8.71％） 、卵
（8.61％）の上昇率が高く、青果物（▲4.24％）、
その他サービス（▲3.88％）のマイナス幅が大
きかった。 
各品目の消費ウエイトも考慮して、全体のイ
ンフレ率への寄与を品目別に見ると（図表 7・8） 、前年比上昇率へのプラス寄与が大きい品目はガ
ソリン（1.02％ポイント） 、家庭サービス（0.61％ポイント）だった。また、前月比上昇率への寄与
では、プラス寄与では教育サービス（約 0.11％ポイント） 、マイナス寄与では青果物（約▲0.20％

### Page 3

原油
(株)日本総合研究所 原油市場展望 2026年10月
◆現状：100ドルを挟んで乱高下
９月のＷＴＩ原油先物価格は、月半ばにかけて
100ドル台後半に上昇。米国とイランの間で攻撃の
応酬が続いたことが背景。加えて、イエメンの武装組
織フーシがバブ・エル・マンデブ海峡付近の港湾都市
を掌握したことや 、サウジアラビアの東西パイプライン
が攻撃を受けて操業を停止したことで 、ホルムズ海
峡を迂回するルートからの石油輸出が停滞するとの
懸念も価格を押し上げ。月末にかけては、一時80ド
ル台後半に下落 。米国とイランの外交交渉が進展
するとの期待や 、東西パイプラインの輸送能力が早
期に回復するとの報道を材料に、中東からの石油供
給の増加が意識されたことが背景。
10月入り後は、90ドル前後で方向感に欠ける展
開。米国の中東への増派が価格を押し上げた一方 、
Ｇ７による石油備蓄放出の合意が価格を下押し。
◆投機筋のネットポジションは小幅に拡大
投機筋の買い越し幅は緩やかに拡大 。中東情勢
の緊迫化を受けて、原油価格の上昇を見込む投資

家が増加している可能性。
◆見通し：当面100ドル前後で高止まり
両国の戦闘がこれ以上激化しないことを前提とす
るメインシナリオでは、原油価格が100ドルを中心と
する高値圏で推移する 見通し。ホルムズ海峡 の通
航制約が価格を下支えする一方、東西パイプライン
などの代替 ルートの活用や、中東産油国の 輸出の
回復が上値を抑えることが背景。

### Page 4

原油
(株)日本総合研究所 原油市場展望 2026年10月3
◆長期化するホルムズ海峡の「封鎖」
足元では、米国とイランの戦闘終結に向けた交渉
が難航しており、ホルムズ海峡の封鎖が継続 。船舶
の位置情報を知らせるＡＩＳのデータに基づく推計
では、同海峡を通航するタンカーの数がほぼゼロに。
◆通常航行が停止しても、原油輸出は増加
もっとも、中東の原油輸出量は足元にかけて緩や
かに増加。海峡封鎖が続くなかでも輸出が回復して
いる背景として、次の２点が指摘可能。
第１に、迂回ルートの活用。サウジアラビアやアラブ
首長国連邦（ＵＡＥ）は、パイプラインを経由して
原油を海峡外のヤンブー港やフジャイラ港に輸送 。
３月以降、これらの港からの輸出が増加。
第２に、タンカー同士の積み替え（ＳＴＳ）の活
用。報道などによると 、ＡＩＳを切って海峡を通過
することで、原油を海峡外に運び出す船舶が存在。
こうした船舶から別の船舶に積み替えられたうえで 、
原油が輸出されている模様 。実際、大規模な迂回
ルートを持たないイラク・クウェートからの輸出や、フー

シの台頭でヤンブー港からの輸出が急減しているサウ
ジアラビアの輸出が、足元にかけて増加。
◆米国・イランの戦闘激化なら、輸出減少も
先行きも、中東の原油輸出は緩やかに回復する
見通し。ただし、輸出回復は限られたパイプラインや
ＡＩＳを停止した船舶による不安定な輸送に依存。
戦闘が激化し、こうした輸送手段が 攻撃されれば、
輸出が再び減少するリスク。

Document 579: 穆迪首席经济学家：伊朗战争致美国家庭平均承担近2000美元成本

Source PDF: all report pdf/穆迪首席经济学家：伊朗战争致美国家庭平均承担近2000美元成本.pdf
Report source: Unknown

### Page 1

穆迪认为⾃今年 2 ⽉爆发以来，伊朗战争已给美国家庭带来平均近 2000 美元的综合成本，
全美总额约 2650 亿美元，主要受能源⼤涨及美联储加息引发的房贷等融资成本攀升驱动。
⽩宫对此测算予以驳斥，强调国家安全诉求。但随着战费每⽉持续增加数以⼗亿计，战事
带来的通胀正显著挤压居⺠实际收⼊。
摘要：
伊朗战争的经济账单正直接落到美国家庭头上。
10 ⽉ 12 ⽇，穆迪分析⾸席经济学家 Mark Zandi 在 X 平台发⽂估算，⾃ 2 ⽉ 28 ⽇美以两国对伊朗发
动打击以来，已给全美带来约 2650 亿美元的综合经济负担，相当于平均每个美国家庭承受近
2000 美元的成本。
在家庭平均承担的近 2000 美元中，能源与交通成本增加约 1100 美元，加息带来的借贷成本上升
约 540 美元，军事⽀出分摊约 360 美元；按当前燃料价格计算，家庭能源⽀出⽬前仍以每周约 50
美元的速度递增。
他写道：
⽩宫迅速对该测算予以驳斥。⽩宫⾸席副新闻秘书 Anna Kelly 质疑 Mark Zandi 的政治⽴场及以往

预测的准确性，并强调特朗普总统采取⾏动旨在确保伊朗⽆法拥有核武器。 Anna Kelly 称：
在美联储上⽉刚刚加息、抵押贷款利率升⾄ 7.5% 的背景下，战争成本如何通过油价、利率与财
政三条渠道继续传导，将直接影响投资者对通胀路径与政策⾛向的判断。
账单拆解：能源、利率与军费
能源与交通成本合计约 1100 美元每⼾，是 Zandi 测算中最⼤的⼀项。Zandi 表⽰：
Zandi 称，战争推升的通胀是美联储上⽉决定加息、并暗⽰将有更多加息的重要原因。他说：
把这场战争的各项成本放在⼀起看，它们已吞噬美国普通家庭过去⼋个⽉收⼊增幅的约⼀半。难
怪这场战争如此不受欢迎。
Mark Zandi 是⺠主党的捐款⼈，⽽且错误频出，包括多次预测从未发⽣的经济衰退。
更⾼的能源价格是成本的最⼤来源。普通汽油⽬前每加仑 4.37 美元，⽽战前还不到 3 美元；柴油
已超过 6 美元，航空燃油价格接近翻倍。随着冬季临近，⼤幅⾛⾼的家庭取暖燃油价格也在推⾼
成本。
⻓期利率也⼤幅上升，固定抵押贷款利率已从战前的不⾜ 6% 攀升⾄ 7.5% 。按我们的测算，更⾼

的利率已令经济付出每⼾约 540 美元的代价。

需要指出的是， Zandi 的数字属于⼴义经济估算，⽽⾮联邦财政⽀出，⼝径明显⾼于布朗⼤学及

据 Newsweek 报道，布朗⼤学估算消费者承担的成本为 1333 亿美元，约合每⼾ 1018 美元；美国
国防部⻓ Pete Hegseth 在作证时给出的直接军事成本约为 375 亿美元。
官⽅成本也在逐⽉累积。美国国会预算办公室（ CBO ） 9 ⽉估算，截⾄ 8 ⽉ 1 ⽇，战争已耗费美军
381 亿美元；若战事持续，每⽉成本预计在 20 亿⾄ 30 亿美元之间，具体取决于冲突强度。
10 ⽉ 5 ⽇，国会研究服务局发布报告，列出战争中的主要装备损失：共有 81 架固定翼或旋翼⻜机
（含⽆⼈机）在冲突中损失或受损。 CBO 估算，截⾄ 8 ⽉ 1 ⽇的装备损失成本在 19 亿⾄ 33 亿美元

之间，具体取决于战争部（ Department of War ）的换装计划。
政治交锋：战争账单成两党攻防焦点
持续的战事正通过价格机制快速向消费端传导。Mark Zandi 在社交平台 X 上指出，战争成本综
合起来已吸收了美国家庭在过去⼋个⽉中约⼀半的收⼊增⻓，直接削弱了消费者的实际购买
⼒。
战争成本已成为⺠主党批评特朗普的主要抓⼿。
9 ⽉， 46 名⺠主党籍及倾向左翼的独⽴派参议员联名致信战争部⻓ Pete Hegseth ，要求提供更多
有关战争成本的信息。
⽩宫⽅⾯则坚持⾏动的正当性，并将⽭头指向⾼⼝径估算的可信度。 Anna Kelly 对 Zandi 党派背
景与预测记录的抨击，正是这⼀回应策略的最新体现。
随着油价居⾼不下、美联储政策路径收紧，战争的经济代价料将继续发酵。这既是美国家庭的
财务负担，也是⽩宫与国会之间的政治变量，更是市场定价⽆法忽视的宏观⻛险。

高資產財富顧問中心
全球市場
策略與展望
 上週美股市場概況：
上週一 (9/9) 美股市場因勞動節休市一天 ，然而美伊消息仍未和緩 ，沙烏地阿拉伯位於吉讚
(Jazan) 的石油設施傳出遭到伊朗襲擊 ，進一步加劇市場對於供給面的擔憂 ；與此同時 ，美國也
隨後公布了 8 月 CPI 與 PPI 數據， PPI 年增升至 5.4%，高於市場預期的 5.3%；CPI 年增持平於
3.4%，但油價仍是主要支撐上漲因素，同時核心 CPI 雖月增上漲 0.3%，然年增仍在走降 2.4%。
數據確定後市場幾乎完全定價 9 月的升息預期。產業方面，受惠油價大漲的能源板塊上漲 1.6%
領跑市場，通訊服務板塊亦在 Meta 等大型股支撐下逆勢收漲 0.5%；資訊科技板塊表現相對抗

跌，微漲0.2%，其中費城半導體指數上漲 0.8%，受 AI 基礎設施與伺服器晶片需求帶動，但軟體
類股則走勢疲弱。領跌方面，醫療保健板塊重摔 3.6% 表現最差，主要受諾華臨床挫折波及生技
族群及安進重挫逾 10% 拖累；受貴金屬與銅價走低影響的原物料板塊下跌1.7%，金融板塊下跌
1.5%，而對利率及油價高度敏感的非必需消費品 （-1.7%）、工業（-1.7%）、房地產（-1.2%）
以及住宅營建相關族群均面臨沉重拋壓 。最終整週三大指數漲跌互見 ，標普 500下跌 0.8% 至
7656.98，納斯達克指數下跌 0.66% 至 226333.04，道瓊工業指下跌 1.57% 至 52573.29。
 本週重點關注：
本週市場核心聚焦於美 、英、日的利率決議；其次 ，中東阿曼塞拉萊會談的 斡旋進展，以及紅海
與曼德海峽的局勢，將直接牽動高企的原油風險溢價與百元油價走勢；最後 ，美國 8 月零售銷售
及歐、日 8 月 CPI 數據將發布；另外留意到本週五將迎來美股 「三巫日」衍生品結算與季報尾聲，

多重不確定性因素恐進一步放大短線盤面的波動。
